"""Does page batching actually help? Time 60 pages three ways.

  whole  - one converter call, page_range 0-59 (no batching)
  serial - three 20-page batches, one worker
"""

import sys
import time
from pathlib import Path

REPO = Path(r"c:\Users\LOQ\Desktop\Projects\Document-Loaders")
sys.path.insert(0, str(REPO / "codes"))

import marker_test as m
from marker.models import create_model_dict


def run() -> None:
    which = sys.argv[1]
    pdf = sorted((REPO / "source").rglob("*.pdf"))[0]

    model_dict = create_model_dict()
    model_dict["inference_manager"].start()
    print(f"server up | mode={which}", flush=True)

    t0 = time.time()
    if which == "whole":
        _, md = m.convert_batch(model_dict, pdf, 0, 59)
        total = len(md)
    else:
        parts = []
        for s, e in [(0, 19), (20, 39), (40, 59)]:
            _, md = m.convert_batch(model_dict, pdf, s, e)
            parts.append(md.rstrip())
            print(f"  [{time.time() - t0:6.1f}s] pages {s}-{e} done", flush=True)
        total = len("\n\n".join(parts))

    print(f"RESULT mode={which} elapsed={time.time() - t0:.1f}s chars={total}", flush=True)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    run()

---

#### 2026-07-20 — Add a marker-clause preprocessing pass ahead of splitting

* Added `preprocessing()` to `chunking/ingest.py`, run on each document's text before `temp_split()`. It targets a specific Marker-conversion defect in RFT-style source PDFs: numbered clauses (e.g. `3.1`, `5.1`) rendered inconsistently as plain paragraphs, dash-prefixed list items, or bold-labelled lines, with a single clause's body wrapped across stray blank lines by source pagination.
* The pass operates on blank-line-delimited blocks and detects runs of 2+ consecutive same-family markers (numeric `x.y`, alphabetic `(a)`, or roman `(i)`), then applies one of two transforms: give every marker in a plain-paragraph sequence the same leading list-syntax character, or promote a bold-labelled/already-headed sequence to matching-level headings with each clause's fragmented body rejoined into one paragraph. A second pass promotes italic `*Label*:` lines that sit under a heading into a one-level-deeper subheading.
* Validated against two hand-authored before/after examples (provided by the user) covering both transform paths; the plain-list case matches exactly, and the heading-promotion case matches after one explicitly agreed simplification (always merge a clause's trailing fragment through to the next marker, rather than special-casing whether that next marker was already heading-formatted in the source). An early version of the heading-promotion path silently dropped content sitting between two markers that neither one's body absorbed (e.g. a nested list under one clause); fixed by carrying that content through verbatim instead of only emitting per-marker segments.
* Sanity-checked against the two real Marker-converted RFT files currently in the local, Git-ignored `marker_results/` corpus: no crashes, one of the two files changed as expected, and the full `ingest.py` pipeline still ran end-to-end.
* Treat this as an experimental, best-effort heuristic rather than a general Markdown normalizer — it is scoped to the numbered-clause/bold-label/italic-sublabel patterns observed in this document family, not arbitrary marker styles.
* Tracked in: uncommitted addition to `chunking/ingest.py`.

---

#### 2026-07-20 (continued) — Validate `preprocessing()` against a full real RFT/contract document

* The user supplied `marker_results/input.md` and `marker_results/expected-output.md` — the full ~2,700-line RFT plus its General Terms and Conditions contract body, converted by Marker, with a hand-produced corrected version — as ground truth superseding the two earlier toy examples. Diffing `preprocessing()`'s output against it surfaced defects the toy examples were too small to expose.
* Fixed a critical bug: alpha (`(a)`) and roman (`(i)`) marker families were scoped globally by a bare `"alpha"`/`"roman"` key, so unrelated enumerations anywhere in the document chained together if their values happened to continue in sequence. This was silently splicing unrelated clauses together (e.g. a Section 6 clause's roman list getting glued after Section 6.20). Fixed by resetting in-progress alpha/roman runs at every heading or numeric-marker boundary.
* Fixed a second, related bug: a short marker sequence nested entirely inside a larger one (e.g. a roman sub-list inside one clause of a heading-promoted run spanning many clauses) produced its own edit whose span sat inside the outer edit's span; applying both scrambled the output. Fixed by dropping fully-nested/overlapping edits and keeping only the outermost one.
* Replaced the "always merge a clause's trailing fragment forward" rule (the simplification agreed after the toy examples) with a sentence-boundary heuristic: only merge the next paragraph fragment while the accumulated text does not yet end in terminal punctuation. The old unconditional rule was actively corrupting output — e.g. merging an entire run of unrelated glossary definitions ("DELAY EVENT", "DISPUTE", "DRAWINGS", "EAD", ...) into a single line. Also extended the (previously heading-branch-only) merge behavior to the plain branch.
* Added a third, previously unspecified transform: Marker glues internal `<span id="...">...</span>` cross-reference anchors to the front of whatever line they annotate, which was breaking heading/list detection for that line. Added `_extract_leading_spans()` to pull them onto their own preceding line(s), run as the last step of `preprocessing()`.
* Diffing against the full ground truth dropped from 1,596 to ~830 changed lines (git-diff style) across these fixes, with the earlier catastrophic corruption eliminated. The remaining diff is dominated by one unresolved ambiguity: "RFT Number: [...]" page-footer boilerplate merges inconsistently in the ground truth itself — sometimes absorbed into the preceding clause regardless of its own punctuation, sometimes left isolated from a following clause even though nothing punctuation-wise distinguishes the two cases. This was flagged to the user rather than further special-cased by guesswork.
* Tracked in: uncommitted changes to `chunking/ingest.py` building on the same-day preprocessing() addition.

---

#### 2026-07-20 (continued) — Fix two `temp_split()` boundary bugs found via the same document

* The user pointed out (via a pasted chunk-run excerpt) that `temp_split()` was splitting clause 5.4's "Technical Bids"/"Commercial Bids" subsections into separate chunks, and splitting clause 5.7 right before its `(a)-(e)` list, even though each clause fits comfortably under `CHUNK_SIZE` as a whole.
* Root cause 1: `_structural_cut`'s heading-hierarchy rule (Rule 2) located a subsection run's closing boundary by searching only for the *next same-level* heading; when a document uses a heading level exactly once (e.g. one-off `#####` subsections inside a single `####` clause), no same-level sibling exists anywhere else in the document, so the search fell back to end-of-document and the rule concluded the run never closes — dropping subsections that actually already fit. Fixed by also accepting the next *shallower* heading as a valid closing boundary.
* Root cause 2: the "keep a list with its lead-in paragraph/heading" helpers required the list to be flat (single indentation level), so a list with any nested sub-item was invisible to the check and fell through to generic size-based cutting. Renamed and relaxed `_flat_list_section_after_heading`/`_flat_list_section_after_paragraph` (now `_list_section_after_*`) to detect lists regardless of nesting, and added `_top_level_item_boundaries()` so the oversized-list fallback splits on top-level pointers only, keeping nested children attached to their parent pointer.
* While verifying with a full round-trip check (every chunk must reappear in the preprocessed text with nothing left over), found that the first version of `_top_level_item_boundaries()` silently dropped content: when the starting cursor was itself mid-way through a nested run (left there by a previous cut), the returned boundaries never included the cursor itself, so everything between it and the next true top-level pointer vanished. Fixed by always forcing the start position in as the first boundary. Confirmed zero dropped content across all four real documents in `marker_results/` after the fix.
* The user then pointed out that clause 5.4's heading and lead-in were still getting stranded at the end of the *previous* chunk while its subsections started the next one — Rule 2 was dropping only the first dropped subsection, not the parent heading that introduces it. Added `_enclosing_heading_start()`: when a subsection run gets pushed forward, the immediately-preceding shallower heading (if it directly owns the run, nothing else in between) is pulled forward with it, so the whole section starts fresh together.
* That exposed a second, latent bug in the same rule: once a subsection hierarchy resolves *before* the size limit (now much more common thanks to the shallower-heading fix above), the "otherwise" branch returned the raw, unsnapped character limit instead of a safe boundary — producing a mid-word cut in unrelated trailing prose that just happened to follow the hierarchy. Fixed by falling through to the existing paragraph/word-boundary rules instead of returning early. Re-verified the round-trip check (still zero dropped content) and the full pipeline on all four documents.
* Tracked in: uncommitted changes to `chunking/ingest.py`.

+import os
+import shutil
+from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor, as_completed
 from pathlib import Path
 
+# surya 0.22 runs the VLM in a separate inference server and picks the backend
+# from the hardware: an NVIDIA GPU selects vllm, which only runs via Docker.
+# Force the native llama.cpp backend instead (needs llama-server on PATH).
+# Must be set before marker imports surya.settings, which reads it at import.
+os.environ.setdefault("SURYA_INFERENCE_BACKEND", "llamacpp")
+
+# A PATH edit never reaches already-running shells, so a terminal older than the
+# llama.cpp install fails with "llama-server binary not found". Point surya at
+# the default install location directly when PATH lookup comes up empty.
+if shutil.which("llama-server") is None:
+    _llama_server = (
+        Path(os.environ.get("LOCALAPPDATA", "")) / "llama.cpp" / "llama-server.exe"
+    )
+    if _llama_server.is_file():
+        os.environ.setdefault("LLAMA_CPP_BINARY", str(_llama_server))
+
+# Guided layout decoding sends a JSON schema that llama.cpp cannot convert to a
+# GBNF grammar (anchored \d{1,4} patterns + integer min/max), so every request
+# fails with "failed to parse grammar". vllm parses it natively; llama.cpp needs
+# it off. surya already ships SURYA_GUIDED_TABLE_REC disabled for a similar
+# reason - the model emits well-formed JSON without the schema.
+os.environ.setdefault("SURYA_GUIDED_LAYOUT", "false")
+
+import pypdfium2
+
 from marker.converters.pdf import PdfConverter
 from marker.models import create_model_dict
 from marker.output import text_from_rendered
@@ -9,32 +37,161 @@ PROJECT_ROOT = Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent
 SOURCE_DIR = PROJECT_ROOT / "source"
 OUTPUT_DIR = PROJECT_ROOT / "marker_results"
 
+# Marker 2.0.0 picks its pipeline from the active device (balanced on CUDA, fast
+# on CPU/MPS), so an unpinned mode makes output depend on whether torch happens
+# to be a CUDA build. Pin it: 'balanced' runs the VLM layout model plus
+# full-page OCR on every page, rather than 'fast', which keeps layout on the
+# rf-detr detector and OCRs only garbled/empty blocks.
+MARKER_MODE = "balanced"
+
+# Optional page batching: split a PDF into BATCH_SIZE-page ranges, convert each
+# through its own PdfConverter, and merge back in page order.
+#
+# OFF by default, because on this stack it only costs. Measured on pages 0-59 of
+# the RFT corpus, balanced mode, RTX 5050 Laptop (8 GB):
+#   whole document, no batching ...... 90.1s
+#   3 x 20-page batches, 1 worker ... 118.7s  (+32%)
+#   3 x 20-page batches, 2 workers .. llama-server aborts, reproducible
+#
+# The slowdown is expected once you see where the parallelism already lives:
+# surya runs one llama-server with SURYA_INFERENCE_PARALLEL slots (default 8)
+# and batches pages across all in-flight requests. One 60-page call keeps those
+# slots saturated; three 20-page calls shrink the pool of pages available to
+# coalesce and re-pay provider/converter setup per batch.
+#
+# Enable it when you want bounded memory or restartability on a very long PDF -
+# not for speed.
+PAGE_BATCHING = False
+BATCH_SIZE = 20
+# Keep at 1. Two document-level workers push concurrent full-page OCR through
+# the shared llama-server and it dies with "bad_variant_access was thrown in
+# -fno-exceptions mode" (a native abort, so no Python traceback - the run just
+# stops). Request-level concurrency is the server's 8 slots, not this.
+MAX_PARALLEL_BATCHES = 1
+
 
 def output_path_for(source_path: Path) -> Path:
     relative_path = source_path.relative_to(SOURCE_DIR)
     return (OUTPUT_DIR / relative_path).with_suffix(".md")
 
 
+def page_count(source_path: Path) -> int:
+    # pypdfium2 is marker's own PDF backend, so this is the same page count the
+    # converter will see - no extra dependency, and no risk of a parser
+    # disagreeing with marker about how many pages the file has.
+    pdf = pypdfium2.PdfDocument(str(source_path))
+    try:
+        return len(pdf)
+    finally:
+        pdf.close()
+
+
+def convert_batch(
+    model_dict: dict,
+    source_path: Path,
+    start_page: int,
+    end_page: int,
+) -> tuple[int, str]:
+    """Convert one inclusive page range. Runs in a worker thread."""
+    # Each batch gets its own PdfConverter: converters carry per-document
+    # builder/processor state and cannot be shared across concurrent workers.
+    # `model_dict` is deliberately shared - in marker 2.0.0 every predictor in
+    # it is a thin client of a shared surya server, and SuryaInferenceManager is
+    # documented "construct once per process". Building a fresh model dict per
+    # worker would create a second manager and race to spawn a second
+    # llama-server against the same GPU.
+    converter = PdfConverter(
+        artifact_dict=model_dict,
+        config={
+            "mode": MARKER_MODE,
+            "page_range": list(range(start_page, end_page + 1)),
+        },
+    )
+    rendered = converter(str(source_path))
+    markdown, _, _ = text_from_rendered(rendered)
+    return start_page, markdown
+
+
+def convert_in_batches(model_dict: dict, source_path: Path) -> str:
+    """Convert a PDF as page batches, merged back into page order.
+
+    Runs MAX_PARALLEL_BATCHES batches at a time; at the default of 1 this is
+    simply sequential.
+    """
+    total_pages = page_count(source_path)
+    batches = [
+        (start, min(start + BATCH_SIZE - 1, total_pages - 1))
+        for start in range(0, total_pages, BATCH_SIZE)
+    ]
+    # flush: this job runs for many minutes and can be killed by a native
+    # llama-server crash, which takes unflushed stdout with it. Progress lines
+    # are only useful if they survive that.
+    print(
+        f"  {total_pages} page(s) in {len(batches)} batch(es), "
+        f"up to {MAX_PARALLEL_BATCHES} concurrent.",
+        flush=True,
+    )
+
+    batch_markdown: dict[int, str] = {}
+    with ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=MAX_PARALLEL_BATCHES) as executor:
+        futures = {
+            executor.submit(
+                convert_batch, model_dict, source_path, start_page, end_page
+            ): (start_page, end_page)
+            for start_page, end_page in batches
+        }
+        for future in as_completed(futures):
+            start_page, end_page = futures[future]
+            # Let a batch failure propagate: a partial document silently missing
+            # 20 pages is worse than a file the batch summary reports as failed.
+            _, markdown = future.result()
+            batch_markdown[start_page] = markdown
+            print(f"  pages {start_page + 1}-{end_page + 1} done", flush=True)
+
+    # as_completed yields in finish order, so reassemble by page offset. Content
+    # spanning a batch boundary (a table, a paragraph) is split by the seam -
+    # batching changes scheduling, not extraction quality.
+    return "\n\n".join(batch_markdown[start_page].rstrip() for start_page, _ in batches)
+
+

     converted = 0

-        print(f"Converting: {source_path.relative_to(PROJECT_ROOT)}")

Read C:\Users\LOQ\AppData\Local\Temp\claude\c--Users-LOQ-Desktop-Projects-Document-Loaders\ef6b1437-8689-40a4-8abe-becfd6d76142\tasks\b8o950sgi.output
I'll stop polling and let the monitor notify me. Summary of where things stand:

What I implemented
Your script now uses balanced mode, counts pages, generates 20-page ranges, runs a converter per batch, merges in page order, and caps concurrency — all in marker_test.py. But I changed four details from the proposal, and measurement contradicted the core premise.

Corrections to the proposal
create_model_dict() per worker → one shared dict. Marker 2.0.0's models.py says every predictor is "a thin client… workers hold NO models," and SuryaInferenceManager is documented "Construct once per process." A dict per worker spawns a competing llama-server — exactly the startup conflict your notes warned about. Converters stay per-worker (they hold per-document state); only the model dict is shared.

pypdf → pypdfium2. pypdf isn't installed; pypdfium2 is, as Marker's own PDF backend. Also uv add pypdf wouldn't have worked — there's no root pyproject.toml (per CLAUDE.md, deps go directly into the venvs). No install needed.

SURYA_INFERENCE_PARALLEL=8 omitted — it's a no-op. DEFAULT_PARALLEL is already 8 in llamacpp.py:72.

Marginalia config omitted. Those values are looser than defaults (header_zone 0.15 vs 0.08, max_chars 300 vs 150), so they strip more aggressively — a content change unrelated to scheduling, and bundling it would make the output diff uninterpretable. Bare keys also leak to any class with a matching attribute; the MarginaliaProcessor_ prefix form is correct here.

The measurement
Pages 0–59, balanced, RTX 5050 (8 GB):

elapsed	outcome
whole document	90.1s	ok
3×20 batches, 1 worker	118.7s	+32% slower
3×20 batches, 2 workers	—	crash, reproduced twice
Two workers abort llama-server with bad_variant_access ... -fno-exceptions — a native abort, so no traceback; the process just dies at 0x80000003. A lighter 2-worker test (3-page batches) passed, so the trigger is sustained concurrent OCR load, not startup.

The slowdown has a cause: surya already parallelizes one layer down. One llama-server with 8 slots coalesces pages across in-flight requests. A 60-page call saturates those slots; three 20-page calls shrink the coalescing pool and re-pay per-batch setup. Document-level threading duplicates that, then oversubscribes it.

So I set PAGE_BATCHING = False and MAX_PARALLEL_BATCHES = 1, keeping the machinery as a documented opt-in for bounded memory on long PDFs — not speed. Flip one flag to re-enable it. Logged as ADR-008 and BUG-016, with Architecture.md updated.

Two things to flag
The full 500-page run is still going — I have not yet confirmed it end-to-end, and Status.md is deliberately unwritten until I do. More importantly, it's already well past what the 60-page benchmark extrapolates to (~6 min/document), which hints whole-document conversion may scale worse than linearly. If so, batching could genuinely win on 250-page files and my default would be wrong for your actual corpus — my numbers only cover 60 pages. I'll report the real figure when it lands.

Also: this switches fast → balanced, so marker_results/ is no longer comparable to prior runs. Per CLAUDE.md, regenerate the whole tree before using it as a baseline.

They're not junk — they're surya's shared model servers, deliberately kept alive:

surya.fast_layout.server on port 52493 (PIDs 17308 → 30172)
surya.ocr_error.server on port 52570 (PIDs 26988 → 27688)
Each is a launcher plus the real server child. Surya spawns these with keep_alive=True specifically so spawn.py does not register an atexit kill (client.py:91-95) — they persist between runs so the models don't reload each time. Killing them is safe; your next run just respawns them and pays the reload cost once.

Robust version (matches by command line, so it works even after PIDs change):


Get-CimInstance Win32_Process -Filter "Name='python.exe'" |
  Where-Object { $_.CommandLine -match 'surya\.(fast_layout|ocr_error)\.server' } |
  ForEach-Object { Stop-Process -Id $_.ProcessId -Force }
Or by exact PID (children first, then launchers — these PIDs are valid as of right now):


Stop-Process -Id 30172, 27688, 17308, 26988 -Force
Verify:


Get-CimInstance Win32_Process -Filter "Name='python.exe'" |
  Select-Object ProcessId, CommandLine
nvidia-smi --query-gpu=memory.used,memory.total --format=csv
If you want maximum free VRAM before a 500-page run, kill them. If you're iterating with repeated runs, leave them — that's what they're there for.

    def __init__(self, filepath: str, config=None):
        super().__init__(filepath, config)

        self.filepath = filepath
        self.raw_pdftext_pages: Dict[int, dict] = {}

        with self.get_doc() as doc:
            self.page_count = len(doc)
            self.page_lines: ProviderPageLines = {i: [] for i in range(len(doc))}
            self.page_refs: Dict[int, List[Reference]] = {
                i: [] for i in range(len(doc))
            }

            if self.page_range is None:
                self.page_range = range(len(doc))

            assert max(self.page_range) < len(doc) and min(self.page_range) >= 0, (
                f"Invalid page range, values must be between 0 and {len(doc) - 1}.  Min of provided page range is {min(self.page_range)} and max is {max(self.page_range)}."
            )

            if self.force_ocr:
                # Manually assign page bboxes, since we can't get them from pdftext
                self.page_bboxes = {i: doc[i].get_bbox() for i in self.page_range}
            else:
                self.page_lines = self.pdftext_extraction(doc)

    @contextlib.contextmanager
    def get_doc(self):
        doc = None
        try:
            doc = pdfium.PdfDocument(self.filepath)

            # Must be called on the parent pdf, before retrieving pages to render correctly
            if self.flatten_pdf:
                doc.init_forms()

            yield doc
        finally:
            if doc:
                doc.close()

    def __len__(self) -> int:
        return self.page_count

        effective_per_slot = ctx_size // max(parallel, 1)
        logger.info(

            f"(~{effective_per_slot}/slot × {parallel} parallel slots)"

Grounded in the code and the vendored libs, here's what's actually going on. Two separate things are bundled in the question, and separating them is most of the answer.

1. It never ran in parallel
marker_test.py:70 pins MAX_PARALLEL_BATCHES = 1. So the 118.7s measurement is sequential batching — three 20-page conversions one after another. The only genuinely parallel configuration you tried (2 workers) didn't produce a slower time; it produced a native abort in llama-server. So there is no "parallel was slower" data point. There's "batching was slower" and "parallel was fatal."

2. Why batching alone costs ~32%
The parallelism you were trying to add already exists one layer down, and it was already saturated.

Marker doesn't run the VLM in-process. It talks to a single llama-server that surya spawns, configured with N slots (llamacpp.py:119). Every predictor call ends in:


chat_completions_batch(batch, ..., max_workers=settings.SURYA_INFERENCE_PARALLEL or DEFAULT_PARALLEL)
batch is the set of pages handed to that call. The thread pool fans them across the server's slots. The unit of parallelism is pages-in-one-call, not documents-in-one-call.

So:

One 60-page call → the pool has 60 items to chew through. All 8 slots stay busy from start to finish, with one ramp-up and one drain at the very end.
Three 20-page calls → three ramp-ups and three drains. At the tail of each batch you're running 7, 5, 3, 1 slots busy while the GPU waits for stragglers before the batch can return. You paid that tail three times instead of once.
That alone accounts for a good chunk of it. On top of that, each batch constructs a fresh PdfConverter, and every converter builds a fresh PdfProvider, whose constructor does this (providers/pdf.py:95-113):


with self.get_doc() as doc:          # pdfium.PdfDocument(filepath) + init_forms()
    self.page_count = len(doc)
    self.page_lines = {i: [] for i in range(len(doc))}   # full doc, not page_range
    ...
It opens and flattens the whole 60-page PDF every time, regardless of page_range. Only pdftext_extraction respects the range. Plus builder and processor construction per batch. Your 28.6s of overhead spread over 2 extra batches is ~14s each — comfortably explained by one full pdfium open + flatten plus a slot-drain tail.

3. Why 2 workers aborted rather than helped
There is one GPU, one llama-server process, one queue. Document-level threads don't create a second execution resource — they just push two concurrent full-page-OCR request streams at a server whose slots were already fully subscribed. Best case that's pure queueing overhead. Actual case it hit a variant-handling bug in llama.cpp compiled -fno-exceptions, so the process aborts with no Python traceback.

The general shape of the mistake
Adding parallelism at layer N only helps if layer N+1 is idle. Here the bottom layer was already at 100% GPU occupancy under a single call, so every unit of "parallelism" you added above it converted directly into overhead: repeated setup, repeated pipeline drain, repeated queue contention.

If you ever want batching for the reasons it's actually good for — bounded memory, restartability on a long PDF — the knob that would recover most of the loss is raising BATCH_SIZE so the drain-tail is amortized over more pages, not raising MAX_PARALLEL_BATCHES. The real throughput knob on this stack is SURYA_INFERENCE_PARALLEL, though on 8 GB you'll hit KV-cache pressure quickly — ctx_size scales as parallel × per_slot.

The comment block at marker_test.py:46-70 already documents this correctly; I'd only add that the provider re-opens the entire PDF per batch, which the current comment doesn't mention.

import os
import shutil
from pathlib import Path

# surya 0.22 runs the VLM in a separate inference server and picks the backend
# from the hardware: an NVIDIA GPU selects vllm, which only runs via Docker.
# Force the native llama.cpp backend instead (needs llama-server on PATH).
# Must be set before marker imports surya.settings, which reads it at import.
os.environ.setdefault("SURYA_INFERENCE_BACKEND", "llamacpp")

# A PATH edit never reaches already-running shells, so a terminal older than the
# llama.cpp install fails with "llama-server binary not found". Point surya at
# the default install location directly when PATH lookup comes up empty.
if shutil.which("llama-server") is None:
    _llama_server = (
        Path(os.environ.get("LOCALAPPDATA", "")) / "llama.cpp" / "llama-server.exe"
    )
    if _llama_server.is_file():
        os.environ.setdefault("LLAMA_CPP_BINARY", str(_llama_server))

# Guided layout decoding sends a JSON schema that llama.cpp cannot convert to a
# GBNF grammar (anchored \d{1,4} patterns + integer min/max), so every request
# fails with "failed to parse grammar". vllm parses it natively; llama.cpp needs
# it off. surya already ships SURYA_GUIDED_TABLE_REC disabled for a similar
# reason - the model emits well-formed JSON without the schema.
os.environ.setdefault("SURYA_GUIDED_LAYOUT", "false")

from marker.converters.pdf import PdfConverter
from marker.models import create_model_dict
from marker.output import text_from_rendered


PROJECT_ROOT = Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent
SOURCE_DIR = PROJECT_ROOT / "source"
OUTPUT_DIR = PROJECT_ROOT / "marker_results"


def output_path_for(source_path: Path) -> Path:
    relative_path = source_path.relative_to(SOURCE_DIR)
    return (OUTPUT_DIR / relative_path).with_suffix(".md")


def main() -> None:
    source_files = sorted(path for path in SOURCE_DIR.rglob("*") if path.is_file())
    if not source_files:
        raise SystemExit(f"No source files found in: {SOURCE_DIR}")

    OUTPUT_DIR.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    # Marker 2.0.0 picks its pipeline from the active device (balanced on CUDA,
    # fast on CPU/MPS), so an unpinned mode makes output depend on whether torch
    # happens to be a CUDA build. Pin it: 'balanced' runs the VLM layout model
    # plus full-page OCR. 'fast' is much quicker but was rejected here - it
    # merged list points together, which chunking then can't recover (ADR-008).
    converter = PdfConverter(
        artifact_dict=create_model_dict(), config={"mode": "balanced"}
    )
    print(f"Marker converter initialized. Found {len(source_files)} source file(s).")

    converted = 0
    failures: list[tuple[Path, Exception]] = []

    for source_path in source_files:
        output_path = output_path_for(source_path)
        output_path.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
        print(f"Converting: {source_path.relative_to(PROJECT_ROOT)}")

        try:
            rendered = converter(str(source_path))
            markdown_output, _, _ = text_from_rendered(rendered)
            output_path.write_text(markdown_output, encoding="utf-8")
            converted += 1
            print(f"Markdown written to: {output_path.relative_to(PROJECT_ROOT)}")
        except Exception as error:
            failures.append((source_path, error))
            print(f"FAILED: {source_path.name}: {error}")

    print(f"Marker complete: {converted} converted, {len(failures)} failed.")
    if failures:
        raise SystemExit(1)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

---

#### 2026-07-21 — Try, measure, and reject parallel page batching for Marker

* Goal was chunk quality, not throughput: strip running headers/footers from Marker's Markdown. `balanced` mode on a 250-page RFT proved slow enough to abandon mid-run, so `fast` mode was tried instead — roughly as quick as before and headers were gone, but it merged separate list points together, which chunking cannot recover. Reverted to `balanced`.
* To make `balanced` affordable, implemented parallel page batching in `codes/marker_test.py`: count pages via `pypdfium2`, convert 20-page `page_range` slices through separate `PdfConverter` instances, merge back in page order. Corrected three details from the original proposal along the way — shared one `create_model_dict()` instead of building one per worker (`SuryaInferenceManager` is documented "construct once per process"; a second dict races to spawn a second llama-server), used `pypdfium2` rather than adding `pypdf` (already a Marker dependency, and there is no root `pyproject.toml` for `uv add`), and dropped `SURYA_INFERENCE_PARALLEL=8` as a no-op equal to the backend's `DEFAULT_PARALLEL`.
* Measured on pages 0–59, `balanced`, RTX 5050 Laptop (8 GB): whole document 90.1s; three 20-page batches serial 118.7s (+32%); three 20-page batches across two workers aborts the llama-server with `bad_variant_access ... -fno-exceptions` — a native abort with no Python traceback, reproduced twice. A two-worker run over *3-page* batches passed, so the trigger is sustained concurrent OCR load rather than startup contention.
* Batching is slower even single-threaded because the parallelism already lives one layer down: surya runs one llama-server with `SURYA_INFERENCE_PARALLEL` (default 8) slots and coalesces pages across in-flight requests, so splitting into 20-page calls shrinks the coalescing pool and re-pays per-batch setup.
* Reverted all batching/parallelism code at the user's direction; `marker_test.py` is back to one converter and a simple sequential loop, now pinned to `balanced`. The original header/footer goal remains unaddressed — the actual lever is `MarginaliaProcessor` config (`MarginaliaProcessor_header_zone` and friends), which is independent of both mode and scheduling.
* Tracked in: ADR-008, BUG-016, `docs/Architecture.md`; `codes/marker_test.py` left with only the pre-existing env-var block plus the `balanced` pin.

---

The thing worth flagging
Nothing in this session moved you toward your actual goal. Header/footer removal was never a mode or scheduling problem — the lever is MarginaliaProcessor, and it's orthogonal to both. Per your CLAUDE.md it takes the ClassName_attr form on the converter config:


config={
    "mode": "balanced",
    "MarginaliaProcessor_header_zone": 0.15,
    "MarginaliaProcessor_max_height_frac": 0.08,
    "MarginaliaProcessor_max_chars": 300,
}
Those loosen the defaults (0.08 / 0.035 / 150) to catch larger headers. I verified the class and attribute names against the installed 2.0.0 during this session. Two cautions: bare keys like header_zone also bind to any other class with that attribute, so prefer the prefixed form; and loosening these makes marginalia stripping more aggressive, which can eat real section headers — worth a short page_range run to check before committing 500 pages to it.

That's a much cheaper experiment than what we just did, and it's the one that actually targets your chunk quality.
